=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given an integer n representing the dimensions of an n x n grid, with the origin at the bottom-left corner of the grid. You are also given a 2D array of coordinates rectangles, where rectangles[i] is in the form [start_x, start_y, end_x, end_y], representing a rectangle on the grid. Each rectangle is defined as follows:

(start_x, start_y): The bottom-left corner of the rectangle.
(end_x, end_y): The top-right corner of the rectangle.

Note that the rectangles do not overlap. Your task is to determine if it is possible to make either two horizontal or two vertical cuts on the grid such that:

Each of the three resulting sections formed by the cuts contains at least one rectangle.
Every rectangle belongs to exactly one section.

Return true if such cuts can be made; otherwise, return false.
 
Example 1:

Input: n = 5, rectangles = [[1,0,5,2],[0,2,2,4],[3,2,5,3],[0,4,4,5]]
Output: true
Explanation:

The grid is shown in the diagram. We can make horizontal cuts at y = 2 and y = 4. Hence, output is true.

Example 2:

Input: n = 4, rectangles = [[0,0,1,1],[2,0,3,4],[0,2,2,3],[3,0,4,3]]
Output: true
Explanation:

We can make vertical cuts at x = 2 and x = 3. Hence, output is true.

Example 3:

Input: n = 4, rectangles = [[0,2,2,4],[1,0,3,2],[2,2,3,4],[3,0,4,2],[3,2,4,4]]
Output: false
Explanation:
We cannot make two horizontal or two vertical cuts that satisfy the conditions. Hence, output is false.

 
Constraints:

3 <= n <= 10^9
3 <= rectangles.length <= 10^5
0 <= rectangles[i][0] < rectangles[i][2] <= n
0 <= rectangles[i][1] < rectangles[i][3] <= n
No two rectangles overlap.

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def checkValidCuts(self, n: int, rectangles: List[List[int]]) -> bool:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

I remember a LeetCode problem "Check if Grid can be Cut into Sections" solution:
```
def check(intervals):
    intervals.sort()
    cnt = 0
    cur_end = -1
    for s,e in intervals:
        if cur_end <= s:
            cnt += 1
            cur_end = e
        else:
            cur_end = max(cur_end, e)
    return cnt >= 3
```

Let's search memory: LeetCode 3394 "Check if Grid can be Cut into Sections" official solution maybe uses:
```
class Solution:
    def checkValidCuts(self, n: int, rectangles: List[List[int]]) -> bool:
        return self._check(rectangles, 0) or self._check(rectangles, 1)
    def _check(self, rectangles, axis):
        intervals = []
        for rect in rectangles:
            intervals.append((rect[axis], rect[axis+2]))
        intervals.sort()
        cnt = 0
        max_end = -1
        for start, end in intervals:
            if max_end <= start:
                cnt += 1
            max_end = max(max_end, end)
        return cnt >= 3
```

So `max_end = max(max_end, end)` works. Count increments when `max_end <= start`. This is common.

Now, let's think about the helper implementation details.

```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def checkValidCuts(self, n: int, rectangles: List[List[int]]) -> bool:
        def can_cut(axis: int) -> bool:
            intervals = []
            for rect in rectangles:
                intervals.append((rect[axis], rect[axis + 2]))
            intervals.sort()
            groups = 1
            max_end = intervals[0][1]
            for start, end in intervals[1:]:
                if max_end <= start:
                    groups += 1
                if end > max_end:
                    max_end = end
            return groups >= 3

        return can_cut(0) or can_cut(1)
```
Where axis=0 for vertical cuts? Wait rectangles = [start_x, start_y, end_x, end_y]. For vertical cuts, we need x intervals: rect[0], rect[2]. For horizontal cuts, y intervals: rect[1], rect[3]. So `can_cut(0)` checks x intervals (vertical cuts), `can_cut(1)` checks y intervals (horizontal cuts). Good.

Need maybe if intervals list empty? rectangles length >=3, so no.

Potential issue: `groups = 1` assumes intervals[0] exists. Yes.

Potential issue: If `max_end <= start` increments groups, but then `max_end = max(max_end, end)`. Suppose intervals [0,5], [5,6], [6,7] groups=3. Good. Suppose intervals [0,5], [5,6], [4,7]? Sorted [0,5],[4,7],[5,6]? Actually [4,7] start4<5, overlap, max_end=7; [5,6] overlap. groups=1. Components: [0,5] overlaps [4,7], [4,7] contains [5,6], all connected. Good.

Let's think about if `max_end <= start` should be `max_end < start`? We already decided <=. Let's double-check with problem examples and definition of overlap. If intervals [0,2] and [2,4] touch, can a horizontal cut at y=2 separate them? Yes. If we used `<`, they would be considered same group (no gap) and count might still be >=3 in some cases? Example 1 horizontal: [0,2],[2,4],[2,3],[4,5] with `<` as overlap? Let's simulate count groups with condition `if max_end < start` (new group only when gap strictly greater):
[0,2] max=2
[2,4] 2<2 false max=4
[2,3] 4<2 false max=4
[4,5] 4<4 false max=5 -> groups=1 false. But example true. So must use `<=`.

Now, let's explore possible counterexamples to the greedy count as connected components under positive overlap.

Greedy algorithm:
```
groups=1
max_end=intervals[0][1]
for s,e in intervals[1:]:
    if max_end <= s:
        groups += 1
    max_end = max(max_end, e)
return groups >= 3
```

Let's use:
```
def can_cut(axis):
    intervals = [(r[axis], r[axis+2]) for r in rectangles]
    intervals.sort()
    groups = 0
    cur_end = -1
    for s,e in intervals:
        if cur_end <= s:
            groups += 1
        if e > cur_end:
            cur_end = e
    return groups >= 3
```
This counts groups. Need check if `cur_end <= s` for first interval with cur_end=-1; since s>=0, true. Good. If intervals could start at -1? no.

But wait: If `cur_end` is -1 and first interval starts at 0, groups=1, cur_end=e. Good.

Now, let's think about if there are intervals [0,
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def checkValidCuts(self, n: int, rectangles: List[List[int]]) -> bool:
        def can_cut(axis: int) -> bool:
            intervals = [(rect[axis], rect[axis + 2]) for rect in rectangles]
            intervals.sort()

            groups = 0
            max_end = -1

            for start, end in intervals:
                if max_end <= start:
                    groups += 1
                max_end = max(max_end, end)

            return groups >= 3

        return can_cut(0) or can_cut(1)
```
